2019 Indian general election

General elections were held in [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India "India") in seven phases from 11 April to 19 May 2019 to elect the members of the [17th Lok Sabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/17th_Lok_Sabha "17th Lok Sabha"). Votes were counted and the result was declared on 23 May.[&#8202;](#ref-back-3-0)[\[1\]](#refnote-3)[&#8202;](#ref-back-4-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-4)[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-5)[&#8202;](#ref-back-6-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-6) Around 912 million people were eligible to vote, and voter turnout was over 67 percent– the highest ever in any Indian general election, as well as the highest ever participation by women voters until the [2024 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indian_general_election "2024 Indian general election").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Jain2019turnout-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-Jain2019turnout)[&#8202;](#ref-back-8-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-8)[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-9)[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[c\]](#refnote-11)

The [Bharatiya Janata Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatiya_Janata_Party "Bharatiya Janata Party") won in a [landslide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landslide_victory "Landslide victory") receiving 37% of the vote, the highest vote share by a political party since the [1989 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Indian_general_election "1989 Indian general election"), and winning 303 seats, further increasing its substantial majority.[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-12) In addition, the BJP-led [National Democratic Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Alliance "National Democratic Alliance") (NDA) won 353 seats. The BJP outperformed most [exit polls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exit_polls "Exit polls") which had predicted a much narrower mandate for the ruling alliance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-13-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-13) The BJP won 37.76%[&#8202;](#ref-back-14-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-14) of votes, while the NDA's combined vote was 45% of the 603.7 million votes that were polled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-15-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-15)[&#8202;](#ref-back-16-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-16) The [Indian National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Congress "Indian National Congress") won 52 seats, failing to get 10% of the seats needed to claim the post of [Leader of the Opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_in_Lok_Sabha "Leader of the Opposition in Lok Sabha").[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-17) In addition, the Congress-led [United Progressive Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Progressive_Alliance "United Progressive Alliance") (UPA) won 91 seats, while other parties won 98 seats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-18)

[Legislative assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vidhan_Sabha "Vidhan Sabha") elections in the states of [Andhra Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Andhra_Pradesh_Legislative_Assembly_election "2019 Andhra Pradesh Legislative Assembly election"), [Arunachal Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Arunachal_Pradesh_Legislative_Assembly_election "2019 Arunachal Pradesh Legislative Assembly election"), [Odisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Odisha_Legislative_Assembly_election "2019 Odisha Legislative Assembly election") and [Sikkim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Sikkim_Legislative_Assembly_election "2019 Sikkim Legislative Assembly election") were held simultaneously with the general election,[&#8202;](#ref-back-19-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-19)[&#8202;](#ref-back-20-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-20) as well as [by-elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%9320_Tamil_Nadu_Legislative_Assembly_by-election "2019–20 Tamil Nadu Legislative Assembly by-election") of twenty-two seats of the Tamil Nadu Legislative Assembly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-21)

## Electoral system

All 543 elected [MPs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_Parliament,_Lok_Sabha "Member of Parliament, Lok Sabha") are elected from single-member constituencies using [first-past-the-post voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First-past-the-post_voting "First-past-the-post voting"). The President of India [appoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Indian_reserved_seats_in_the_Lok_Sabha "Anglo-Indian reserved seats in the Lok Sabha") an additional two members from the [Anglo-Indian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Indian "Anglo-Indian") community if he believes that community is under-represented.[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-22)

Eligible voters must be Indian citizens, 18 or older, an ordinary resident of the polling area of the constituency and registered to vote (name included in the electoral rolls), possess a valid [voter identification card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_ID_(India) "Voter ID (India)") issued by the [Election Commission of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission_of_India "Election Commission of India") or an equivalent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-23) Some people convicted of electoral or other offences are barred from voting.[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-24)

The elections are held on schedule and as per the [Constitution of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India "Constitution of India") that mandates parliamentary elections once every five years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-25)

## Election schedule

[![Election Dates of Indian General Election, 2019](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/2019_Lok_Sabha_Election_Schedule.svg/250px-2019_Lok_Sabha_Election_Schedule.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2019_Lok_Sabha_Election_Schedule.svg) Election schedule

The election schedule was announced by [Election Commission of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission_of_India "Election Commission of India") (ECI) on 10 March 2019, and with it the [Model Code of Conduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission_of_India's_Model_Code_of_Conduct "Election Commission of India's Model Code of Conduct") came into effect.[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-26)

The election was scheduled to be held in seven phases. In Bihar, Uttar Pradesh and West Bengal, the election was held in all seven phases. The polling for the [Anantnag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anantnag_(Lok_Sabha_constituency) "Anantnag (Lok Sabha constituency)") constituency in the state of Jammu and Kashmir was held in three phases, due to violence in the region.[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-27)[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-28)

3. [&#8202;](#refnote-:12x)[↑](#ref-back-:12x-0)Polling in [Tripura East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripura_East_(Lok_Sabha_constituency) "Tripura East (Lok Sabha constituency)") was rescheduled from 18 to 23 April.

### Rescheduled voting, cancellations

- [Vellore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vellore_(Lok_Sabha_constituency) "Vellore (Lok Sabha constituency)"), [Tamil Nadu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_Nadu "Tamil Nadu"): Over ₹11 crore (US$1.1million) in cash was seized in Vellore from [DMK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravida_Munnetra_Kazhagam "Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam") leaders – a regional party in Tamil Nadu. According to _[The News Minute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_News_Minute "The News Minute")_, this cash is alleged to have been for [bribing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_bribery "Political bribery") the voters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-32) Based on the evidence collected during the raids, the Election Commission of India cancelled the 18 April election date in the [Vellore constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vellore_(Lok_Sabha_constituency) "Vellore (Lok Sabha constituency)"). The DMK leaders denied wrongdoing and alleged a conspiracy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-33)
- [Tripura East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripura_East_(Lok_Sabha_constituency) "Tripura East (Lok Sabha constituency)"), [Tripura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripura "Tripura"): The [Election Commission of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission_of_India "Election Commission of India") deferred polling from 18 to 23 April due to the law and order situation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-34) The poll panel took the decision following reports from the Special Police Observers that the circumstances were not conducive for holding free and fair elections in the constituency.[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-35)

## Campaign

Main article: [Campaigning in the 2019 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaigning_in_the_2019_Indian_general_election "Campaigning in the 2019 Indian general election")

### Issues

#### Allegations of undermining institutions

The opposition parties accused the NDA government of destroying democratic institutions and processes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-36-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-36) Modi denied these allegations, and blamed Congress and the communists for undermining institutions including the police, the CBI, and the CAG, and cited the [murder of BJP activists in Kerala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Kerala#The_epicenter_of_violence "Politics of Kerala") and Madhya Pradesh.[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-37) The Congress party, along with other opposition parties and a group of retired civil servants, accused the ECI of being compromised, and implied that they endorsed the model code of conduct violations by Narendra Modi and other BJP political leaders during their campaigns.[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-38)\[ _[verification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\] Another group of 81 retired civil servants, judges and academics disputed these allegations, made counter-allegations, and stated that the ECI acted fairly and similarly in alleged violations by either side. The group stated that such political attacks on the ECI were a "deliberate attempt to denigrate and delegitimise the democratic institutions".[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-39)\[ _[verification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\]

#### Economic performance

According to _[The Times of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Times_of_India "The Times of India")_, the major economic achievements of the incumbent [NDA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Alliance_(India) "National Democratic Alliance (India)") government included an inflation rate less than 4 per cent, the [GST reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_(India) "Goods and Services Tax (India)"), and the [Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insolvency_and_Bankruptcy_Code,_2016 "Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016"). Its programs, in recent years, that have positively touched many among the Indian masses, include the [Jan Dhan Yojana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Dhan_Yojana "Jan Dhan Yojana"), rural cooking gas and electricity for homes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-toiApril1420192-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-toiApril1420192) According to the [IMF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund "International Monetary Fund"), the [Indian economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_economy "Indian economy") has been growing in recent years, its GDP growth rate is among the highest in the world for major economies, and India is expected to be the fastest growing major economy in 2019–2020 and 2020–2021, with real GDP projected to grow at 7.3 per cent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-41)[&#8202;](#ref-back-42-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-42)[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-43) The GDP growth data has been disputed[&#8202;](#ref-back-toiApril1420192-1)[\[34\]](#refnote-toiApril1420192) by a group of Indian social scientists, economists and the political opposition's election campaign, while a group of Indian chartered accountants has defended the data, the GDP calculation methodology, and questioned the motivations of those disputing the recent Indian GDP statistics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-44)

The opposition's election campaign has claimed that both the [demonetisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Indian_banknote_demonetisation "2016 Indian banknote demonetisation") and GST law have "seriously hit small business, farmers and casual labour", states _The Times of India_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-toiApril1420192-2)[\[34\]](#refnote-toiApril1420192)[&#8202;](#ref-back-45-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-45) The incumbent has claimed that they inherited a country from the previous Congress-led government that was "a legacy of policy paralysis, corruption and economic fragility", and that the BJP-led government policies have placed India on better economic fundamentals and a fast gear.[&#8202;](#ref-back-et04042019b2-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-et04042019b2) Modi claims that his government pursued demonetisation in the national interest, his government has identified and de-registered 338,000 shell companies, identified and recovered ₹130,000 crore (US$13billion) in black money since 2014, and almost doubled India's tax base.[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-47)[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-48) The Congress party disputes the incumbents' claims, and has alleged that BJP offices have "become hubs of creating black money", and seeks a judicial inquiry into the Rafale deal with France and BJP's role in corruption.[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-49)

#### National security and terrorism

Further information: [India–Pakistan border skirmishes (2019)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93Pakistan_border_skirmishes_(2019) "India–Pakistan border skirmishes (2019)") and [2019 Balakot airstrike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Balakot_airstrike "2019 Balakot airstrike")

In response to the [2019 Pulwama attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Pulwama_attack "2019 Pulwama attack"), the [Indian Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Air_Force "Indian Air Force") conducted [airstrikes inside Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Balakot_airstrike "2019 Balakot airstrike") — for the first time since the [1971 Indo-Pakistani war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pakistani_War_of_1971 "Indo-Pakistani War of 1971"). The [ongoing conflict with Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93Pakistan_border_skirmishes_(2019) "India–Pakistan border skirmishes (2019)") became a significant factor in the election. The opposition parties accused of politicising the army, whilst the BJP countered their accusations by stating that such allegations raised by them were adversely affecting the morale of armed forces.[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-50)

According to the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center "Pew Research Center"), both before and after the outbreak of recent India-Pakistan tensions, their 2018 and 2019 surveys suggest that the significant majority of the voters consider Pakistan as a "very serious threat" to their country, and terrorism to be a "very big problem".[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-51)[&#8202;](#ref-back-pewMarch252019a2-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-pewMarch252019a2)

#### Unemployment

Main article: [Unemployment in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unemployment_in_India "Unemployment in India")

According to the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center "Pew Research Center"), a majority of Indian voters consider the lack of employment opportunities as a "very big problem" in their country. "About 18.6 million Indians were jobless and another 393.7 million work in poor-quality jobs vulnerable to displacement", stated the Pew report.[&#8202;](#ref-back-pewMarch252019a2-1)[\[46\]](#refnote-pewMarch252019a2)

A report on unemployment prepared by the National Sample Survey Office's (NSSO's) periodic labour force survey, has not been officially released by the government. According to _[Business Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_Today_(India) "Business Today (India)")_, this report is the "first comprehensive survey on employment conducted by a government agency after Prime Minister Narendra Modi announced demonetisation move in November 2016". According to this report, the 2017–2018 "usual status"[&#8202;](#ref-back-jobs-0)[\[d\]](#refnote-jobs) unemployment rate in India is 6.1 per cent, which is a four-decade high.[&#8202;](#ref-back-54-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-54)[&#8202;](#ref-back-jobs-1)[\[d\]](#refnote-jobs) The government has claimed that the report was not final.[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-59) According to the [International Labour Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Labour_Organization "International Labour Organization") (ILO) – a United Nations agency, unemployment is rising in India and the "unemployment rate in the country \[&#8202;](#ref-back-ilojan20192-0)[India\] will stand at 3.5 percent in 2018 and 2019 – the same level of unemployment seen in 2017 and 2016", instead of dropping to 3.4 per cent as it had previously projected.[\[53\]](#refnote-ilojan20192) According to the ILO's World Employment Social Outlook Report, the unemployment rate in India has been in the 3.4 to 3.6 per cent range over the UPA-government led 2009–2014 and the NDA-government led 2014–2019 periods.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ilojan20192-1)[\[53\]](#refnote-ilojan20192)

Opposition parties claimed in their election campaign that the unemployment in India had reached crisis levels. The NDA government has denied the existence of any job crisis.[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-61) Prime minister Narendra Modi claimed that jobs are not lacking but the accurate data on jobs has been lacking.[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-62)[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-63)

The opposition has attacked the NDA government's performance with the NSSO reported 6.1 per cent unemployment data. Modi and his government have questioned this job statistics report, stating that "most surveys that try to capture unemployment rate are skewed since these did not cover the unorganised sector, which accounts for 85–90 per cent of jobs \[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[in India\]".[\[57\]](#refnote-64)

#### Agrarian and rural distress

The Congress party campaign highlighted "agrarian distress" as an election issue.[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-65) The BJP campaign highlighted that the Congress party had been in power for five generations of the [Nehru dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nehru%E2%80%93Gandhi_family "Nehru–Gandhi family") and its past promises and campaign issues have been empty. It claimed that the recent farmer loan waivers by Congress have not reached "even 10% of the farmers" nor has it helped the financial situation of the farmers. BJP highlights that its "Kisan Samman Nidhi" helps the small farmers at the time of seed planting through a direct deposit of ₹6000 to their accounts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-66) The opposition accused this as being an attempt to lure voters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-67)

According to _The Times of India_, a group of farmer associations demanded that the 2019 election manifesto of competing political parties should promise to "keep agriculture out of the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization "World Trade Organization") (WTO)" and that the interests of Indian farmers must not be compromised in global trade treaties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-toi031520192-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-toi031520192) They also demanded loan waivers and income support for the agriculture sector.[&#8202;](#ref-back-toi031520192-1)[\[61\]](#refnote-toi031520192) According to the _[Business Standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_Standard "Business Standard")_ and the United Nations' [Food and Agriculture Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization "Food and Agriculture Organization"), India has witnessed record crop harvests in recent years including 2017 when its farmers grew more foodgrains than ever before.[&#8202;](#ref-back-buNov3020182-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-buNov3020182)[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-70) However, the farmers consider the "low remunerative prices" they receive in the free market to be too low and a need for the Indian government to establish higher minimum support prices for agricultural products. These farmers consider this an issue for the 2019 general elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-buNov3020182-1)[\[62\]](#refnote-buNov3020182)

#### Dynasty politics

The BJP highlighted that the Congress party has relied on [Rahul Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahul_Gandhi "Rahul Gandhi") for leadership since 2013, its lack of internal party institutions and claimed that whenever Congress has been in power, the freedom of press and Indian government institutions have "taken a severe beating".[&#8202;](#ref-back-it032020192-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-it032020192)[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-72) During the election campaign, its leaders mentioned the Emergency of 1975, the nepotism, corruption and widespread abuses of human rights under the Congress rule in the past.[&#8202;](#ref-back-it032020192-1)[\[64\]](#refnote-it032020192)[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-73)[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-74) Congress-led alliance leader [H. D. Kumaraswamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._D._Kumaraswamy "H. D. Kumaraswamy") – the son of a former prime minister of India and the former chief minister of Karnataka, countered that "India developed because of dynasty politics", stating that "dynasty politics are not the main issue, rather country's problems are".[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-75) The Congress alleged hypocrisy by the BJP, claiming that the BJP itself forms alliances with dynasty-based parties such as the [Akali Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiromani_Akali_Dal "Shiromani Akali Dal") in Punjab, and that family relatives of senior BJP leaders such as [Rajnath Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajnath_Singh "Rajnath Singh") and [Arun Jaitley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arun_Jaitley "Arun Jaitley") have been in politics too.[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-76)

According to an IndiaSpend report published by the BloombergQuint, the smaller and regional parties such as the Jammu and Kashmir National Conference, Lok Jan Sakti Party, Shiromani Akali Dal, Biju Janata Dal and Samajwadi Party have higher densities of dynasty-derived candidates and elected representatives in recent years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bq032920192-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-bq032920192)[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-78) While both the Congress and the BJP have also nominated candidates from political dynasties, states the report, the difference between them is that in Congress "top party leadership has been handed down from generation to generation within the same \[&#8202;](#ref-back-bq032920192-1)[Nehru Gandhi dynasty\] family", while there has been a historic non-dynastic diversity in the top leadership within the BJP. According to the report, while BJP has also nominated candidates from political dynasties, its better public relations operation "can leap to its defence when attacked on the same grounds".[\[70\]](#refnote-bq032920192) In contrast to the IndiaSpend report, analysis of [Kanchan Chandra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanchan_Chandra "Kanchan Chandra"), a prominent professor of Politics, of the 2004, 2009 and 2014 general elections included a finding that the Congress party has had about twice or more dynastic parliamentarians than the BJP at those elections, and higher than all major political parties in India except the Samajwadi Party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chandra2016p152-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-Chandra2016p152)[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[e\]](#refnote-81) Many of these dynastic politicians in India who inherit the leadership positions have never held any jobs and lack state or local experience, states Anjali Bohlken – a professor and political science scholar, and this raises concerns of rampant nepotism and appointments of their own friends, relatives and cronies if elected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chandra2016p15-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-Chandra2016p15) The BJP targeted the Congress party in the 2019 elections for alleged nepotism and a family dynasty for leadership.[&#8202;](#ref-back-it032020192-2)[\[64\]](#refnote-it032020192)[&#8202;](#ref-back-bq032920192-2)[\[70\]](#refnote-bq032920192)

### Campaign controversies

#### Income tax raids

In April 2019, raids conducted by the [Income Tax Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_Tax_Department "Income Tax Department") found bundles of unaccounted for cash amounting to ₹281 crore (US$29million), along with liquor and documentary evidence in premises of people with close connections to Madhya Pradesh chief minister [Kamal Nath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamal_Nath "Kamal Nath") of the Congress. Modi has highlighted this evidence to attack the Congress in its election campaign, alleging corruption is part of Congress party's culture.[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-82)[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-83)

#### Social media abuses and fake news

According to _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")_ and _[The Wall Street Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wall_Street_Journal "The Wall Street Journal")_, the election attracted a systematic attempt to spread misinformation through social media.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wsj04012019a2-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-wsj04012019a2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt04012019a2-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-nyt04012019a2) [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook") said that over a hundred of these advocacy accounts spreading disinformation were traced to "employees of the [Pakistani military public relations wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-Services_Public_Relations "Inter-Services Public Relations")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-wsj04012019a2-1)[\[76\]](#refnote-wsj04012019a2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt04012019a2-1)[\[77\]](#refnote-nyt04012019a2) Some others have been linked to the INC and BJP.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wsj04012019a2-2)[\[76\]](#refnote-wsj04012019a2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt04012019a2-2)[\[77\]](#refnote-nyt04012019a2)

Political parties spent over ₹53 crore (US$5.5million) with the largest spending by BJP on digital platforms for online ads. The BJP placed 2,500 ads on Facebook while the Congress placed 3,686 ads.[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-86) According to a study by Vidya Narayanan and colleagues at the [Oxford Internet Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_Internet_Institute "Oxford Internet Institute"), social media was used by all the major parties and alliances, and all of them linked or posted divisive and conspiratorial content and images. According to Narayanan, "a third of the BJP's images, a quarter of the INC's images, and a tenth the SP-BSP's images were catalogued as divisive and conspiratorial".[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-87)[&#8202;](#ref-back-oii051320192-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-oii051320192) The Narayanan et al. study added that "we observed very limited amounts of hate speech, gore or pornography in either platform samples" by BJP, Congress or SP-BSP, but the election did include proportionally more polarising information on social media than other countries except for the US presidential election in 2016.[&#8202;](#ref-back-oii051320192-1)[\[80\]](#refnote-oii051320192)

About 50,000 fake news stories were published during the recent Lok Sabha elections and shared 2 million times, according to a study conducted by fact-checking startup Logically.[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-89)

In September 2019, the [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC") launched the [Trusted News Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trusted_News_Initiative "Trusted News Initiative") to help combat election-related disinformation, citing the 2019 general elections as a motivating factor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:13-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-:13)

#### ECI actions under Article 324

The [Election Commission of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission_of_India "Election Commission of India") (ECI) curtailed West Bengal's campaigning by one day, after a bust of 19th century Bengali icon [Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishwar_Chandra_Vidyasagar "Ishwar Chandra Vidyasagar") was vandalised due to violence in the 7th phase of the election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-91)

### Party campaigns

Main articles: [Bharatiya Janata Party campaign for the 2019 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatiya_Janata_Party_campaign_for_the_2019_Indian_general_election "Bharatiya Janata Party campaign for the 2019 Indian general election") and [Indian National Congress campaign for the 2019 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Congress_campaign_for_the_2019_Indian_general_election "Indian National Congress campaign for the 2019 Indian general election")

- 12 January 2019 – Prime Minister Modi launched the BJP's election campaign.[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-92)
- 14 February 2019 – The INC president Rahul Gandhi launched his campaign from Lal Dungri village in Gujarat's [Dharampur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharampur,_Gujarat "Dharampur, Gujarat").[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-93)
- 24 March 2019 – The [Aam Aadmi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aam_Aadmi_Party "Aam Aadmi Party") began its campaign in Delhi.[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-94)[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-95)
- 2 April 2019 – The [Trinamool Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinamool_Congress "Trinamool Congress") party launched its campaign from Dinhata, Coochbehar.[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-96)
- 7 April 2019 – [Bahujan Samaj Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahujan_Samaj_Party "Bahujan Samaj Party") and [Samajwadi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samajwadi_Party "Samajwadi Party") began campaigning together as an alliance ( _[Mahagathbandhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahagathbandhan_(Uttar_Pradesh) "Mahagathbandhan (Uttar Pradesh)")_) along with regional parties such as the [Rashtriya Lok Dal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashtriya_Lok_Dal "Rashtriya Lok Dal").[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-97) Their first joint campaign started in [Deoband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deoband "Deoband") in [Saharanpur district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saharanpur_district "Saharanpur district") of [Uttar Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh "Uttar Pradesh").[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-98)[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-99)

### Party manifestos

#### Highlights of the Congress manifesto

The Congress released its manifesto, titled _Congress Will Deliver_ on 3 April.[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-100) Some of its highlights:[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-101)[&#8202;](#ref-back-itApril82019a2-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-itApril82019a2)

- Introduce a _[Nyuntam Aay Yojana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nyuntam_Aay_Yojana "Nyuntam Aay Yojana")_ welfare program wherein ₹72,000 (US$750) per year will be transferred directly to the bank account of a woman-member in each family in the poorest 20 per cent households.
- Create 1 million "Seva Mitra" jobs in rural and urban local government bodies. Fill all 400,000 central government vacancies before March 2020, and encourage state governments to fill their 2,000,000 vacancies. Enact a law that requires all non-government controlled employers with over 100 employees to implement an apprentice program.
- Enact a permanent National Commission on Agricultural Development and Planning and introduce a "Kisan Budget" (Farmer Budget) in the parliament every year. Waive all farmer loans in all states with any amounts outstanding.
- Enact a Right to Homestead Act that will provide free land to every household that does not own a home.
- Enact a Right to Healthcare Act and guarantee every citizen free diagnostics, free medicines, free hospitalisation, and free out-patient care. Double spending on healthcare to 3 per cent of its GDP by 2024.
- Double spending on education to 6 per cent of its GDP by 2024.
- Revise the national GST law from three tax tiers to a single moderate rate of tax. Reduce taxes on exported products to zero. Exempt from the GST essential goods and services that are currently not exempt. Enact a new Direct Taxes Code.
- Augment and rapid construction of national highways. Modernise Indian railway infrastructure. Promote green energy. Manufacturing promotion.
- Increase defence spending.
- Enact a National Election Fund, wherein public funds will be distributed to recognised political parties to run their campaign
- Preserve special status and special rights to natives of Jammu and Kashmir under Article 370 and 35A.[&#8202;](#ref-back-itApril82019a2-1)[\[94\]](#refnote-itApril82019a2)
- Amend the [Armed Forces (Special Powers) Act, 1958](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces_(Special_Powers)_Act "Armed Forces (Special Powers) Act"). End the Sedition law ( [Section 124A of the Indian Penal Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_124A_of_the_Indian_Penal_Code "Section 124A of the Indian Penal Code")).

#### Highlights of the BJP manifesto

The BJP released its manifesto sub-titled _Sankalpit Bharat, Sashakt Bharat_ (lit.'Resolute India, Empowered India') on 8 April.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bjpApril820192-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-bjpApril820192) Some of its highlights:[&#8202;](#ref-back-itApril82019a2-2)[\[94\]](#refnote-itApril82019a2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-bjpApril820192-1)[\[95\]](#refnote-bjpApril820192)[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-104)

- Implementation of a nationwide [National Register of Citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Citizens "National Register of Citizens") (NRC)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Wire_21_Dec_CAA-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-Wire_21_Dec_CAA) to identify & deport [undocumented immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_immigration_to_India "Illegal immigration to India"), an immigrant being defined in this context as a person who is unable to provide documentary evidence of his/her residency in India prior to 26 March 1971 or that of his/her immediate ancestors (parents & grandparents) in case of being born after the previously mentioned date, preceded by an [amendment in citizenship laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizenship_(Amendment)_Act,_2019 "Citizenship (Amendment) Act, 2019") that will allow only undocumented Hindu, Sikh, Buddhist, Jain, Parsi, and Christian immigrants from Pakistan, Bangladesh, and Afghanistan who entered India before 31 December 2014 to automatically obtain Indian citizenship.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bjplsmanifesto-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-bjplsmanifesto)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ITEndorsement-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-ITEndorsement)
- [End the special autonomous status of Jammu and Kashmir and the special rights granted to its citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revocation_of_the_special_status_of_Jammu_and_Kashmir "Revocation of the special status of Jammu and Kashmir") as per [Article 370](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_370_of_the_Constitution_of_India "Article 370 of the Constitution of India") of the [Indian constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_India "Constitution of India").[&#8202;](#ref-back-itApril82019a2-3)[\[94\]](#refnote-itApril82019a2)
- Double farmer incomes by 2022 via the completion of major and micro-irrigation infrastructure projects, open adequate markets and modern farm produce storage centres, implement minimum price supports for farmer produce, farmer loans, and construct of all-weather rural roads. Introduce a pension bill for small and marginal farmers to provide social security after 60 years of age.
- Bring all secondary schools under the national board quality purview. Invest ₹100,000 crore (US$10billion) in higher education, open new and increase seats at existing engineering, management and law schools. Establish skills and innovations centre at block-level in every town. Enhance higher education opportunities for women by introducing financial support and subsidies programs. Source 10 per cent of government procurement from companies with more than 50 per cent female employees.
- Ensure a _pucca_ (lit.'Solid'or 'Durable') house, with safe potable water, toilet, LPG gas cylinder, electricity, and banking account for every family in the country. Reduce the percentage of families living under the poverty line to a single digit by 2024.
- Double the length of national highways. Improve fuel quality by mandating 10 per cent ethanol in petrol. Scale renewable energy capacity to 175 GW.
- Electrify and convert to broad gauge all railway tracks.
- Establish 150,000 health and wellness centres. Start 75 new medical colleges. Raise doctor-to-population ratio to 1:1400. Triple childcare facilities. Achieve 100 per cent immunisation of all babies.
- Raise India's ranking further in " [ease of doing business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ease_of_doing_business_index "Ease of doing business index")". Double exports, introduce single-window compliance procedures for all businesses.
- Reduce air pollution by eliminating all [crop residue burning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crop_residue_burning "Crop residue burning").
- Digitise paperwork and proceedings, modernise the courts.
- Launch and promote a National Digital Library with e-books and leading journals to provide free knowledge accessible to all students. Launch a "Study in India" program to bring foreign students to institutes of higher education.
- Privatisation of defence, space and agriculture sector for development of India.
- Zero tolerance for terrorism, fund resources to strengthen national security, guarantee veterans and soldier welfare, modernise police forces.

#### Other parties

Other national and regional parties released their manifestos too:

- The [Tamil Nadu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_Nadu "Tamil Nadu")-based regional parties [AIADMK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIADMK "AIADMK") and [DMK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravida_Munnetra_Kazhagam "Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam") released their manifesto on 18 March 2019, with each promising to release the seven Tamils jailed after being found guilty for their role in the assassination of [Rajiv Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi "Rajiv Gandhi"), a former Congress party leader and prime minister of India. The AIADMK promised to press for the political rights of the Tamil people in the Eelam region of [Sri Lanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lanka "Sri Lanka"), while the DMK has promised Indian citizenship to all Sri Lankan expats. According to the Deccan Herald, the AIADMK has promised a cash transfer of ₹18,000 (US$190) per year to "all families below the poverty level, destitute women, widows without income, differently-abled, landless agricultural labourers, rural and urban manual labourers and destitute senior citizens". The AIADMK also promised to raise the tax exemption limit and revisions to the GST law. The DMK promised a probe into [Rafale fighter jet deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafale_deal_controversy "Rafale deal controversy"), and a plan to distribute free [sanitary napkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanitary_napkins "Sanitary napkins") to working women along with starting martial arts schools for girls.[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-108)
- [Biju Janata Dal (BJD)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biju_Janata_Dal "Biju Janata Dal") released its manifesto on 9 April 2019. It promised a ₹100,000 (US$1,000) zero-interest crop loan to farmers every year, a ₹500,000 (US$5,200) zero-interest loan to women-run self-help groups, 75 per cent jobs reservation in [Odisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odisha "Odisha")-based companies to Odisha youth, free education to all girls and a marriage assistance grant of ₹25,000 (US$260) to daughters of poor families. It also promised to complete two expressways.[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-109)
- [Communist Party of India (Marxist)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_India_(Marxist) "Communist Party of India (Marxist)") (CPIM) manifesto promised to raise the minimum wage to ₹216,000 (US$2,200) per year, an old age pension of ₹72,000 (US$750) per year and universal public distribution of 35 kilograms of foodgrains per family. It also stated the restoration of inheritance tax and an increase in the taxes on individuals and corporations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cnbc032920192-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-cnbc032920192) It also promised spending 6 per cent of GDP on education, enacting a Right to Free Health Care with 3.5 per cent of GDP on health in the short term and 5 per cent in the long term, introduction of price controls on essential drugs, breaking monopoly of drug multinationals, as well as enact a Right to Guaranteed Employment in urban areas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-111)
- [Nationalist Congress Party (NCP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalist_Congress_Party "Nationalist Congress Party") promised to open talks with Pakistan on terrorism. It also promised to expand trade and political relationship with Russia, and seek to weaken Russia's ties with China and Pakistan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-112)
- [Samajwadi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samajwadi_Party "Samajwadi Party") promised an annual pension of ₹36,000 (US$370) to poor families in a form of a cash transfer to women. It has also proposed a new property tax of 2 per cent on homes valued above ₹25,000,000 (US$260,000) as well as raising income taxes on the affluent. It also promised to create 100,000 new jobs every year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-113-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-113)
- [Telugu Desam Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telugu_Desam_Party "Telugu Desam Party") released its manifesto on 5 April 2019. It promised zero-interest loans to farmer without any caps, a grant of ₹15,000 (US$160) per year to each farmer as investment support, a grant of ₹100,000 (US$1,000) to each family with a daughter in the year of her marriage, an unemployment allowance of ₹3,000 (US$31) for any youth who has completed intermediate education, and free laptops to all students at the intermediate level.[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-114)
- [AITMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Trinamool_Congress "All India Trinamool Congress")'s manifesto was released on 27 March 2019. It promised a judicial probe into demonetisation, a review of GST law, and sought to bring back the Planning Commission. It also promised free medical care, expanding the "100-day work scheme" currently operating in India to "200-day work scheme" along with a pay increase.[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-115)
- [Aam Aadmi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aam_Aadmi_Party "Aam Aadmi Party") released its manifesto on 25 April 2019 promising full statehood for [Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi "Delhi") to give the [Delhi government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_government "Delhi government") control over police and other institutions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-116) The manifesto promised 85 per cent reservations in the Delhi-based colleges and jobs for the voters of Delhi and their families.[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-117)[&#8202;](#ref-back-118-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-118)

### Campaign finance

Several organisations offered varying estimates for the cost of the election campaign. The Centre for Media Studies in New Delhi estimated that the election campaign could exceed $7 billion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-et041420192-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-et041420192) According to the [Association for Democratic Reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_for_Democratic_Reforms "Association for Democratic Reforms") (ADR), an election watchdog, in the financial year 2017–18 BJP received ₹4,370,000,000 (US$45million), about 12 times more donations than Congress and five other national parties combined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-et041420192-1)[\[111\]](#refnote-et041420192)

The [electoral bonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_bond "Electoral bond") in denominations ranging from 1,000 rupees to 10 million rupees ($14 to $140,000) can be purchased and donated to a political party. The bonds don't carry the name of the donor and are exempt from tax.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bb031620192-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-bb031620192)[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[f\]](#refnote-124) Factly – an India data journalism portal, traced the electoral bond donations for 2018 under India's Right to Information Act. According to Factly, electoral bonds worth about ₹10,600,000,000 (US$110million) were purchased and donated in 2018. According to Bloomberg, this accounted for 31.2 per cent of political donations in 2018, while 51.4 per cent of the total donated amount were each below ₹20,000 (US$210) and these too were from unknown donors. About 47 per cent of the donations to political parties were from known sources.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bb031620192-1)[\[112\]](#refnote-bb031620192) Between 1 January and 31 March 2019, donors bought ₹17,100,000,000 (US$180million) worth of electoral bonds and donated.[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-125) The spending in elections boosts national GDP, and the 2009 election spending contributed about 0.5 per cent to GDP.[&#8202;](#ref-back-126-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-126)

According to the Centre for Media Studies, the BJP spent over ₹280 billion (or 45%) of the ₹600 billion spent by all political parties during the polls.[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-127) Congress questioned the BJP over its poll expenditure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-128)

## Parties and alliances

### Political alliances

Main articles: [National Democratic Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Alliance "National Democratic Alliance"), [United Progressive Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Progressive_Alliance "United Progressive Alliance"), and [Communism in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism_in_India "Communism in India")

With the exception of 2014, no single party has won the majority of seats in the Lok Sabha since 1984, and therefore, forming alliances is the norm in Indian elections.

There were three main national pre-poll alliances. They are the National Democratic Alliance (NDA) headed by the BJP, the United Progressive Alliance (UPA) headed by the INC and the [Left Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism_in_India#Alliances "Communism in India") of the communist leaning parties.

The INC did not form alliances in states where it was in direct contest with the BJP. These states included Himachal Pradesh, Uttarakhand, Rajasthan, Gujarat, Madhya Pradesh, and Chhattisgarh. It formed alliances with regional parties in Jammu and Kashmir, Bihar, Tamil Nadu, Maharashtra, Karnataka, Jharkhand, and Kerala.[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-129)

The left parties, most notably the [Communist Party of India (Marxist)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_India_(Marxist) "Communist Party of India (Marxist)") contested on its own in its strongholds [West Bengal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Bengal "West Bengal"), [Tripura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripura "Tripura") and [Kerala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerala "Kerala"), confronting both NDA and UPA. In Tamil Nadu, it was part of the [Secular Progressive Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_Progressive_Alliance "Secular Progressive Alliance") led by [DMK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravida_Munnetra_Kazhagam "Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam") while it was allied with the [Jana Sena Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jana_Sena_Party "Jana Sena Party") in Andhra Pradesh.[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-130)

In January 2019, [Bahujan Samaj Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahujan_Samaj_Party "Bahujan Samaj Party") and [Samajwadi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samajwadi_Party "Samajwadi Party") announced a grand alliance ( _[Mahagathbandhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahagathbandhan_(Uttar_Pradesh) "Mahagathbandhan (Uttar Pradesh)")_) to contest 76 out of the 80 seats in [Uttar Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uttar_Pradesh "Uttar Pradesh") leaving two seats, namely [Amethi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amethi_Lok_Sabha_constituency "Amethi Lok Sabha constituency") and [Rae Bareli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raebareli_Lok_Sabha_constituency "Raebareli Lok Sabha constituency"), for INC and another two for other political parties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto2-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-auto2)

### Political parties

More than 650 parties contested in these elections. Most of them were small with regional appeal. The main parties were the Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP), the Indian National Congress (INC) and the Communist Party of India (Marxist) (CPI(M)). This was the first time that BJP (437) contested more seats than Congress (421) in the Lok Sabha elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-:0)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-:1)

## Candidates

Main articles: [List of National Democratic Alliance candidates in the 2019 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_National_Democratic_Alliance_candidates_in_the_2019_Indian_general_election "List of National Democratic Alliance candidates in the 2019 Indian general election") and [List of United Progressive Alliance candidates in the 2019 Indian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_Progressive_Alliance_candidates_in_the_2019_Indian_general_election "List of United Progressive Alliance candidates in the 2019 Indian general election")

Altogether 8,039 candidates were in the fray for 542 parliamentary constituencies, i.e., 14.8 candidates per constituency on an average, according to PRS India, an NGO.[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-134)

About 40% of the candidates fielded by the Bharatiya Janata Party had a criminal case against them. The key opposition party Indian National Congress was not far behind with 39% of the candidates having criminal charges while the proportion exceeded 50% for some political parties, according to the Association of Democratic Reforms analysis.[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-135)

Parties and alliances contesting for the 2019 electionsshow

## Voter statistics

According to the ECI, 900 million people were eligible to vote, with an increase of 84.3 million voters since the last election in 2014,[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-136)[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-137) making it the largest-ever election in the world.[&#8202;](#ref-back-138-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-138) 15 million voters aged 18–19 years became eligible to vote for the first time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-139)[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-140) 468 million eligible voters were males, 432 million were females and 38,325 identified themselves belonging to [third gender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_gender "Third gender"). Total 71,735 overseas voters also enrolled.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

The residents of the former [enclaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enclave_and_exclave "Enclave and exclave") exchanged under the [2015 India-Bangladesh boundary agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India%E2%80%93Bangladesh_enclaves "India–Bangladesh enclaves") voted for the first time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-141)

### Electronic voting machines and security

The ECI deployed a total of 1.74 million [voter-verified paper audit trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter-verified_paper_audit_trail "Voter-verified paper audit trail") (VVPAT) units and 3.96 million [electronic voting machines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_voting_in_India "Electronic voting in India") (EVM) in 1,035,918 polling stations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-142)[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-143)[&#8202;](#ref-back-144-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-144)[&#8202;](#ref-back-145-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-145) Approximately 270,000 paramilitary and 2 million state police personnel provided organisational support and security at various polling booths.[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-146) On 9 April 2019, the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_India "Supreme Court of India") ordered the ECI to increase VVPAT slips vote count to five randomly selected EVMs per assembly constituency, which meant that the ECI had to count VVPAT slips of 20,625 EVMs before it could certify the final election results.[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-147)[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-148)[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-149)

### Voting

In the first phase, 69.58 per cent of the 142 million eligible voters cast their vote to elect their representatives for 91 Lok Sabha seats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-:52) The [voter turnout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voter_turnout "Voter turnout") was 68.77 per cent in the same constituencies in the 2014 general elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-1)[\[141\]](#refnote-:52) In the second phase, 156 million voters were eligible to vote for 95 Lok Sabha seats and the turnout was 69.45 per cent, compared to 69.62 per cent in 2014.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-2)[\[141\]](#refnote-:52) For the third phase, 189 million voters were eligible to elect 116 Lok Sabha representatives.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-3)[\[141\]](#refnote-:52) According to ECI, the turnout for this phase was 68.40 per cent, compared to 67.15 per cent in 2014.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-4)[\[141\]](#refnote-:52) In the fourth of seven phases, 65.50 per cent of the 128 million eligible voters cast their vote to elect 72 representatives to the Indian parliament while the turnout for the same seats in the 2014 election was 63.05 per cent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:52-5)[\[141\]](#refnote-:52) The fifth phase was open to 87.5 million eligible voters, who could cast their vote in over 96,000 polling booths.[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-151) In the sixth phase, 64.40 per cent of the 101 million eligible voters cast their vote in about 113,000 polling stations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-152)

## Turnout

The final turnout stood at 67.11 per cent, the highest ever turnout recorded in any of the general elections till date. The percentage is 1.16 per cent higher than the 2014 elections whose turnout stood at 65.95 per cent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Jain2019turnout-1)[\[5\]](#refnote-Jain2019turnout) Over 600 million voters polled their votes in 2019 Indian General elections.

## Surveys and polls

### Opinion polls

This section is an excerpt from [Opinion polling for the 2019 Indian general election § Opinion polling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling_for_the_2019_Indian_general_election#Opinion_polling "Opinion polling for the 2019 Indian general election").\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Opinion_polling_for_the_2019_Indian_general_election&action=edit)\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Opinion_polling_for_the_2019_Indian_general_election.svg/500px-Opinion_polling_for_the_2019_Indian_general_election.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Opinion_polling_for_the_2019_Indian_general_election.svg) Number of seats projected in opinion polls per alliance over time.[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/IndianGeneralElectionTrend.jpg/500px-IndianGeneralElectionTrend.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:IndianGeneralElectionTrend.jpg) Indian General Election Trends - 2019

Various organisations carried out [opinion polling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_polling "Opinion polling") to gauge voting intentions in India. The results of such polls are displayed in this list. The date range for these opinion polls is from the [previous general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indian_general_election "2014 Indian general election"), held in April and May 2014, to the present day. The ECI banned the release of [exit polls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exit_poll "Exit poll") from 11 April to 19 May, the last phase of the elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-156-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-156) The commission also banned the publication or broadcast in the media of predictions made by astrologers and tarot card readers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-157)

| [NDA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Alliance_(India) "National Democratic Alliance (India)") | [UPA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Progressive_Alliance "United Progressive Alliance") |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| [Opinion polls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_poll "Opinion poll") | 8 April 2019 | Times Now-VMR | 279 | 149 | 115 | 7 | [&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-158) |

## Aftermath

### Reactions

#### National

Congress party leaders, such as [Rahul Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahul_Gandhi "Rahul Gandhi") and others, conceded defeat and congratulated Modi and his party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-186) Other opposition parties and political leaders such as [Sharad Pawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharad_Pawar "Sharad Pawar"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-187) [Mamata Banerjee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamata_Banerjee "Mamata Banerjee"), and [Omar Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Abdullah "Omar Abdullah"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-188) congratulated Modi and the BJP for their victory.

On 20 November 2019, the [Association for Democratic Reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_for_Democratic_Reforms "Association for Democratic Reforms") filed a petition with the [Supreme Court of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_India "Supreme Court of India") over alleged ballot-counting discrepancies in the Lok Sabha voting and seeking a probe by the ECI.[&#8202;](#ref-back-189-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-189)

### Government formation

This section is an excerpt from [Timeline of the 2019 Indian general election § Government formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election#Government_formation "Timeline of the 2019 Indian general election").\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election&action=edit)\]

- 23 May 2019: PM [Narendra Modi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narendra_Modi "Narendra Modi") wins a second term with an overwhelming majority.
- 30 May 2019: PM [Narendra Modi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narendra_Modi "Narendra Modi") is sworn in as [Prime Minister of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_India "Prime Minister of India") with 24 Cabinet Ministers, 9 MoS (Independent Charge) and 24 MoS.[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-191)

### Swearing-in ceremony

This section is an excerpt from [Second oath of office ceremony of Narendra Modi § lead section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_oath_of_office_ceremony_of_Narendra_Modi#lead_section "Second oath of office ceremony of Narendra Modi").\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Second_oath_of_office_ceremony_of_Narendra_Modi&action=edit)\]

[Narendra Modi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narendra_Modi "Narendra Modi"), parliamentary leader of the [Bharatiya Janata Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatiya_Janata_Party "Bharatiya Janata Party"), started his tenure after his oath of office as the 16th [Prime Minister of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_India "Prime Minister of India") on 30 May 2019. Several [other ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Modi_ministry "Second Modi ministry") were also sworn in along with Modi. The ceremony was noted by media for being the first ever oath of office of an Indian Prime Minister to have been attended by the heads of all [BIMSTEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BIMSTEC "BIMSTEC") countries.

### Impact

The benchmark [BSE Sensex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sensex "Sensex") and [Nifty50](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNX_Nifty "CNX Nifty") indices hit intraday record highs and the [Indian rupee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_rupee "Indian rupee") strengthened after the exit polls and on the day the election results were announced.[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-192)

## Timeline

This section is an excerpt from [Timeline of the 2019 Indian general election § Electoral events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election#Electoral_events "Timeline of the 2019 Indian general election").\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election&action=edit)\]

Electoral timelines are as below:[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)

### March 2019

- **10 March 2019: The [Election Commission of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission_of_India "Election Commission of India") announced election scheduled to the [17th Lok Sabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/17th_Lok_Sabha "17th Lok Sabha")**.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-1)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
  - The [Model Code of Conduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission_of_India's_Model_Code_of_Conduct "Election Commission of India's Model Code of Conduct") comes into force.[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-194)
- 18 March 2019: Issue of notification for the 1st poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-2)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 19 March 2019: Issue of notification for the 2nd poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-3)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 25 March 2019: Last date for filing nominations for the 1st poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-4)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 26 March 2019: Last date for filing nominations for the 2nd poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-5)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
  - Scrutiny of nominations filed for the 1st poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-6)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 27 March 2019: Scrutiny of nominations filed for the 2nd poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-7)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 28 March 2019: Issue of notification for the 3rd poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-8)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
  - Last day for withdrawal of candidature filed for the 1st poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-9)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 29 March 2019: Last day for withdrawal of candidature filed for the 2nd poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-10)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)

### April 2019

- 2 April 2019: Issue of notification for the 4th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-11)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- **3 April 2019:** **[Indian National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_National_Congress "Indian National Congress") released their manifesto titled _Congress Will Deliver_.**[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-195)
- 4 April 2019: Last date for filing nominations for the 3rd poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-12)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 5 April 2019: Scrutiny of nominations filed for the 3rd poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-13)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- **8 April 2019: [Bharatiya Janata Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bharatiya_Janata_Party "Bharatiya Janata Party") released its manifesto titled _Sankalpit Bharat, Sashakt Bharat_**.[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-196)[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-197)
  - Last day for withdrawal of candidature filed for the 3rd poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-14)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 9 April 2019: Last date for filing nominations for the 4th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-15)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 10 April 2019: Issue of notification for the 5th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-16)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
  - Scrutiny of nominations filed for the 4th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-17)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- **11 April 2019: Polling held at 91 parliamentary constituencies spanning over 20 states for the first poll day.**[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-18)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 12 April 2019: Last day for withdrawal of candidature filed for the 4th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-19)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 16 April 2019: Issue of notification for the 6th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-20)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- **18 April 2019: Polling held at 95 parliamentary constituencies spanning over 13 states for the second poll day.**[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-21)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI) ( [Vellore constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vellore_(Lok_Sabha_constituency) "Vellore (Lok Sabha constituency)") election cancelled due to illegal cash deposit from [Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dravida_Munnetra_Kazhagam "Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam") candidate.)\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]
  - Last date for filing nominations for the 5th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-22)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 20 April 2019: Scrutiny of nominations filed for the 5th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-23)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 22 April 2019: Issue of notification for the 7th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-24)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
  - Last day for withdrawal of candidature filed for the 5th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-25)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- **23 April 2019: Polling held at 117 parliamentary constituencies spanning over 14 states for the third poll day.**[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-26)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
  - Last date for filing nominations for the 6th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-27)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 24 April 2019: Scrutiny of nominations filed for the 6th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-28)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 26 April 2019: Last day for withdrawal of candidature filed for the 6th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-29)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- **29 April 2019: Polling held at 71 parliamentary constituencies spanning over 9 states for the fourth poll day.**[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-30)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
  - Last date for filing nominations for the 7th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-31)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- 30 April 2019: Scrutiny of nominations filed for the 7th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-32)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)

### May 2019

- 2 May 2019: Last day for withdrawal of candidature filed for the 7th poll day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-33)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- **6 May 2019: Polling held for 51 parliamentary constituencies in more than 7 states for the fifth polling day.**[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-34)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- **12 May 2019: Polling held for 59 parliamentary constituencies in more than 7 states the sixth polling day.**[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-35)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- **19 May 2019: Polling held at 59 parliamentary constituencies in more than 8 states the seventh polling day.**[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-36)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)
- **23 May 2019: Counting of votes and declaration of results for all polling days.**[&#8202;](#ref-back-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI-37)[\[181\]](#refnote-Timeline_of_the_2019_Indian_general_election_ECI)

## Notes

1. [&#8202;](#refnote-1)[↑](#ref-back-1-0)The election for the [Vellore constituency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vellore_(Lok_Sabha_constituency) "Vellore (Lok Sabha constituency)") was delayed and took place on 5 August 2019.
2. [&#8202;](#refnote-seats)[↑](#ref-back-seats-0)Two seats were reserved for Anglo-Indians and filled through Presidential nomination.
3. [&#8202;](#refnote-11)[↑](#ref-back-11-0)In 9 states and union territories of India – such as [Arunachal Pradesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arunachal_Pradesh "Arunachal Pradesh"), [Kerala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerala "Kerala") and [Uttarakhand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uttarakhand "Uttarakhand") – more women turned out to vote than men in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-10)
4. [&#8202;](#refnote-jobs)[Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-jobs-0) [2](#ref-back-jobs-1)The unemployment data in India is not collected on a monthly or an annual basis, rather it is determined through a sample survey once every 5 years, with a few exceptions. The survey methodology is unlike those in major world economies, and sub-classifies unemployment into categories such as "usual status unemployment" and "current status unemployment" based on the answers given by the individuals interviewed. Its methodology and results have been questioned by various scholars.[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-55)[&#8202;](#ref-back-bhalla04132019-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-bhalla04132019)[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-57) The report and the refusal of the BJP government to release it has been criticised by economist [Surjit Bhalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surjit_Bhalla "Surjit Bhalla").[&#8202;](#ref-back-bhalla04132019-1)[\[49\]](#refnote-bhalla04132019) According to Bhalla, the survey methodology is flawed and its results absurd, because the sample survey-based report finds that India's overall population has declined since 2011–12 by 1.2 per cent (contrary to the Census data which states a 6.7 per cent increase). The report finds that India's per cent urbanisation and urban workforce has declined since 2012, which is contrary to all other studies on Indian urbanisation trends, states Bhalla.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bhalla04132019-2)[\[49\]](#refnote-bhalla04132019) According to NSSO's report's data, "the Modi government has unleashed the most inclusive growth anywhere, and at any time in human history" – which is as unbelievable as the unemployment data it reports, states Bhalla.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bhalla04132019-3)[\[49\]](#refnote-bhalla04132019) The NSSO report suggests the inflation-adjusted employment income of casual workers has dramatically increased while those of the salaried wage-earners has fallen during the 5-years of BJP government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bhalla04132019-4)[\[49\]](#refnote-bhalla04132019) The NSSO has also changed the sampling methodology in the latest round, state Bhalla and Avik Sarkar,[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-58) which is one of the likely sources of its flawed statistics and conclusions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bhalla04132019-5)[\[49\]](#refnote-bhalla04132019)
5. [&#8202;](#refnote-81)[↑](#ref-back-81-0)According to Chandra: in 2009 after the persistently dynastic Samajwadi party, the larger Biju Janata Dal ranked next, followed by the Congress party. In 2004 and 2014, Congress ranked second.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chandra2016p15-1)[\[73\]](#refnote-Chandra2016p15)
6. [&#8202;](#refnote-124)[↑](#ref-back-124-0)Stanley Kochanek in 1987 published about the "briefcase politics" tradition in Indian politics during the decades when the Congress party dominated Indian national politics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-121) Similarly, Rajeev Gowda and E Sridharan in 2012 have discussed the history of campaign financing laws in India and the role of black money in Indian elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-122) Devesh Kapur and Milan Vaishnav discuss the rise of "briefcase" black money donations in India triggered by the 1969 campaign financing bans proposed and enacted by Indira Gandhi, and the campaign finance law reforms thereafter through 2017. They call the recent reforms as yielding "greater transparency than ever before, though limited".[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-123)
7. [&#8202;](#refnote-153)[↑](#ref-back-153-0)![Increase](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b0/Increase2.svg/20px-Increase2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)/![Decrease](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Decrease2.svg/20px-Decrease2.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail) indicate change from the 2014 elections.
8. [&#8202;](#refnote-154)[↑](#ref-back-154-0)Polling in Anantnag was scheduled over three days.
9. [&#8202;](#refnote-155)[↑](#ref-back-155-0)Tamil Nadu has 39 constituencies. Polling in Vellore was cancelled and later held on 5 August 2019.
